Equation

E.26
in the proof of
Lemma

3.5
. This initialization removes the need for the first while loop in (lines 4–6).
We now prove that Algorithm
1

achieves the required precision and
is asymptotically correct.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
p_{\min}\in(0,1)
and
3.6
and
3.7
hold. If
\lambda_{n}^{\star}(p_{\min})<\infty
, then
\lambda_{n}^{\star}(p_{\min})
can be approximated with arbitrary precision by the binary search
Algorithm

1
, that is,
\mathrm{Binary.Search}(N,p_{\min})-\lambda_{n}^{\star}(p_{\min})\to 0,
as
N\to\infty
.

3.6
Algorithm and consistency

The dual representation of the PULSE estimator is not guaranteed to be well-defined in the over-identified setup.
In particular, it is not well-defined if the TSLS is outside the interior of the acceptance region (which corresponds to a
p-value of less than or equal to
p_{\min}
). In this case, we propose to output a warning.
This can be
helpful information for the user since it may indicate a model misspecification.
For example, if the true relationship is in fact nonlinear, and one considers
an over-identified
case (e.g., by
constructing different
transformations of the instrument),
even the TSLS may be rejected when
erroneously considering a linear model; see
Keane 2010
and
Mogstad and
Wiswall 2010
.
For any
p_{\min}\in(0,1)
we
can still define an always well-defined
modified PULSE estimator

\hat{\alpha}_{\text{PULSE}+}^{n}(p_{\min})

as

\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{PULSE}}^{n}(p_{\min})
if the dual representation is feasible and some other
consistent estimator
\hat{\alpha}^{n}_{\text{ALT}}
(such as
the TSLS, LIML or Fuller estimator) otherwise.
That is, we define

\displaystyle\hat{\alpha}_{\text{PULSE}+}^{n}(p_{\min}):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{PULSE}}^{n}(p_{\min}),&\text{if }T_{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\text{TSLS}}^{n})<Q_{\chi^{2}_{q}}(1-p_{\min})\\
\hat{\alpha}^{n}_{\text{ALT}},&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

Similarly to the case of an empty rejection region,
we also output a warning
for the case when the OLS estimator is accepted. This may, but does not have to, indicate weak instruments.
Thus, we have the algorithm presented as
Algorithm

2
in
Algorithm

2
for computing the PULSE
+
estimator.

We now prove that the PULSE
+
estimator consistently estimates the causal parameter in the just- and over-identified setting.
Assume that we choose
a consistent
estimator

\hat{\alpha}_{\text{ALT}}^{n}

(under standard regularity assumptions, this is satisfied for the TSLS).
8
8

8

This holds as

\hat{\alpha}_{\text{TSLS}}^{n}=\alpha_{0}+(n^{-1}\mathbf{Z}^{\intercal}\mathbf{A}(n^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{A})^{-1}n^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z})^{-1}n^{-1}\mathbf{Z}^{\intercal}\mathbf{A}(n^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{A})^{-1}n^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{U}_{Y}
.

We can then show that, under mild conditions, the PULSE
+
estimator, too, is a consistent estimator of
\alpha_{0}
.

Theorem 3.4

(Consistency of PULSE
+
)
.

Consider the just- or over-identified setup and let
p_{\min}\in(0,1)
. If
3.4
,
3.6
,
3.7
,
3.8
and
3.9
hold almost surely for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{ALT}}^{n}
consistently estimates
\alpha_{0}
, then
\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{PULSE}+}^{n}(p_{\min})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\longrightarrow}}\alpha_{0}
, when
n\to\infty
.

We believe that a similar statement also holds in the under-identified setting, see
Section

H.3
.

4
Simulation experiments

In
Appendix

H
we conduct an extensive simulation study investigating the finite sample behaviour of the PULSE estimator. The concept of weak instruments is central to our analysis. An introduction to weak instruments can be found in
Appendix

J
. Here we give a brief overview of the study and the observations.

4.1
Distributional robustness

The
theoretical results on distributional robustness
proved in
Section

2

translate to finite data. The experiments of
Section

H.1
shows that
even for small sample sizes, K-class estimators outperform
both OLS and TSLS for a certain range of interventions, matching the theoretical predictions with increasing sample size. In
section

H.3
, we furthermore consider an under-identified setting.

4.2
Estimating causal effects

When focusing on the estimation of a
causal effect in an identified setting,
our simulations show
that there are several settings where PULSE outperforms the Fuller and TSLS estimators in terms of mean squared error (MSE).
In univariate simulation experiments, such settings are
characterized by
weakness of instruments and weak confounding (endogeneity).
The characterization becomes more involved
in multivariate settings,
but is similar in that PULSE outperforms all other methods for small confounding strengths, an effect amplified by the weakness of instruments.
Below we detail the univariate simulation setup and refer the reader to
Appendix

H
for further details and the multivariate simulation experiments mentioned above.

4.2.1
Univariate model

We first
compared performance measures of the estimators in a univariate instrumental variable model. As seen in
Hahn and
Hausman 2002
and
Hahn
et al. 2004
, we consider structural equation models of the form

\displaystyle A:=A\in\mathbb{R}^{q},\quad X:=A^{\intercal}\bar{\xi}+U_{X}\in\mathbb{R},\quad Y:=X\gamma+U_{Y}\in\mathbb{R},

where
A\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I)
and
A\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}(U_{X},U_{Y})
with

\begin{pmatrix}U_{X}\\
U_{Y}\end{pmatrix}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}1&\rho\\
\rho&1\end{pmatrix}\right).

Furthermore, we let
\gamma=1
and
\bar{\xi}^{\intercal}=(\xi,....,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
, where
\xi>0
is chosen according to the theoretical
R^{2}
-coefficient. We consider the following simulation scheme: for each
q\in\{1,2,3,4,5,10,20,30\}
,
\rho\in\{0.1,0.2,...,0.9\}
,
R^{2}\in\{0.0001,0.001,0.01,0.1,0.3\}
and
n\in\{50,100,150\}
, we simulate
n
-samples from the above system and calculate the OLS, TSLS, Fuller(1), Fuller(4) and PULSE (
p_{\min}=0.05
) estimates; see
Section

H.2.1
.

Figure

2
contains illustrations of the relative change in square-root mean squared error (RMSE) estimated from
15000
repetitions. On the horizontal axis we have plotted the average first stage F-test as a measure of weakness of instruments; see
Appendix

J
for further details. A test for
H_{0}:\bar{\xi}=0
, i.e., for the relevancy of instruments,
at a significance level of 5%, has different rejection thresholds in the range
[1.55,4.04]
depending on
n
and
q
. The vertical dashed line corresponds to the smallest rejection threshold of 1.55 and the dotted line corresponds to the ‘rule of thump’ threshold of 10. Note that the lowest possible negative relative change is
-1
and a positive relative change means that PULSE is better.

Figure 2:
Illustrations of the relative change in RMSE.

In
Appendix

K
, further illustrations of e.g. the relative change in mean bias and variance of the estimators are presented.
We also conducted the simulations for setups with combinations of
\gamma\in\{-1,0\}
, components of
\bar{\xi}
chosen negatively, with random flipped sign in each coordinate and for negative
\rho
(not shown but available in the folder ’Plots’ in the code repository). The results with respect to MSE are similar to those shown in
Figure

2
, while the bias comparison changes depending on the setup.

We observe that there are settings, in which the PULSE is superior to TSLS, Fuller(1) and Fuller(4) in terms of MSE.
This is particularly often the case in weak instrument settings (
\hat{E}_{N}(G_{n})<10
) for low confounding strength
(\rho\leq 0.2)
. Furthermore, as we tend towards the weakest instrument setting considered, we also see a gradual shift in favour of PULSE for higher confounding strengths. In these settings with weak instruments and low confounding we also see that OLS is superior to the PULSE in terms of MSE. However, for large confounding setups PULSE is superior to OLS in terms of both bias and MSE and this superiority increases as the instrument strength increases.
The PULSE is generally more biased than the Fuller and TSLS estimators but less biased than OLS. However, in the settings with weak instruments and low confounding the bias of PULSE and OLS is comparable.
In summary, the PULSE is in these settings more biased but its variance is so small that it is MSE superior to the Fuller and TSLS estimators.

5
Empirical applications

We now consider three classical
instrumental variable applications (see
Albouy 2012
and
Buckles and
Hungerman 2013
for discussions on the underlying assumptions).

(i)

“Does compulsory school attendance affect schooling and earnings?” by
Angrist and
Krueger 1991
. This paper investigates the effects of education on wages. The endogenous effect of education on wages are remedied by instrumenting education on quarter of birth indicators.

(ii)

“Using geographic variation in college proximity to estimate the return to schooling” by
Card 1993
. This paper also investigates the effects of education on wages. In this paper education is instrumented by proximity to college indicator.

(iii)

“The colonial origins of comparative development: An empirical investigation” by
Acemoglu
et al. 2001
. This paper investigates the effects of extractive institutions (proxied by protection against expropriation) on the gross domestic product (GDP) per capita. The endogeneity of the explanatory variables are remedied by instrumenting protection against expropriation on early European settler mortality rates.

We have
applied the different estimators OLS, TSLS, PULSE, and Fuller to the classical data sets

Acemoglu
et al. 2001
,
Angrist and
Krueger 1991
and
Card 1993
.
All models considered in
Angrist and
Krueger 1991
and
Card 1993
, where we estimate the effect on years of education on wages, using quarter of birth and proximity to colleges as instruments, respectively,
the OLS estimates are not rejected by our test statistic and
PULSE outputs the OLS estimates; see
Appendix

I
for futher details.
This may be either due to weak endogeneity (weak confounding), or that the test has insufficient power to reject the OLS estimates due to either weak instruments or severe over-identification.

5.1
Acemoglu
et al. 2001

The dataset of
Acemoglu
et al. 2001
consists of 64
observations,
each corresponding to a different country for which mortality rate estimates encountered by the first European settlers are available. The endogenous target of interest is log GDP per capita (in 1995). The main endogenous regressor in the dataset is an index of expropriation protection (averaged over 1985–1995), i.e., protection against expropriation of private investment by the respective governments. The average expropriation protection is instrumented by the settler mortality rates. We consider eight models M1–M8 which correspond to the models presented in column (1)–(8) in Table 4 of
Acemoglu
et al. 2001
. Model M1 is given by the reduced form structural equations

\displaystyle\log\mathrm{GDP}=\text{avexpr}\cdot\gamma+\mu_{1}+U_{1},\quad\text{avexpr}=\log\mathrm{em4}\cdot\delta+\mu_{2}+U_{2},

where avexpr is the average expropriation protection, em4 is the settler mortality rates,
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
are intercepts and
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
are possibly correlated, unobserved noise variables. In model M2 we additionally introduce an included exogenous regressor describing the country latitude. In model M3 and M4 we fit model M1 and M2, respectively, on a dataset where we have removed Neo-European countries, Australia, Canada, New Zealand and the United States. In model M5 and M6 we fit model M1 and M2, respectively, on a dataset where we have removed observations from the continent of Africa. In model M7 and M8 we again fit model M1 and M2, respectively, but now also include three exogenous indicators for the continents Africa, Asia and other.

Table

1
shows the OLS and TSLS estimates
(which replicate the values from the study),
as well as the Fuller(4) and PULSE estimates for the linear effect of the average expropriation protection on log GDP. In model M1, for example, we see that the PULSE estimate suggests that the average expropriation risk linear effect on log GDP is 0.6583 which is 26% larger than the OLS estimate but 34% smaller than TSLS estimate. In models M5–M8, the OLS estimates are not rejected by the Anderson-Rubin test, so the PULSE estimates coincide with the OLS estimates.

.

We can also use this example to
illustrate the robustness property of K-class estimators;
see
Theorem

2.1
.
Even though interventional data are not available,
we can consider the mean squared prediction error when
holding out the observations with the most extreme values of the instrument.
Depending on the degree of generalization, we indeed see that
the PULSE and Fuller tend to outperform OLS or TSLS in terms of mean squared prediction error on the held out data; see
Section

I.3
for further details.

6
Summary and future work

We have proved that a distributional robustness property similar to the one shown for anchor regression
(
Rothenhäusler et al. 2021
)

fully extends to general K-class estimators of possibly non-identifiable structural parameters in a general linear structural equation model that allows for latent endogenous variables.
We have further proposed a novel estimator for structural parameters in linear structural equation models. This estimator, called PULSE, is derived as the solution to a minimization problem, where we seek to minimize mean squared prediction error constrained to a
confidence region
for the causal parameter. Even though this region is non-convex, we have shown
that the corresponding optimization problem allows for a computationally efficient algorithm
that approximates the above parameter with arbitrary precision using a simple binary search procedure.
In the under-identified setting, this estimator extends existing work in the machine learning literature that considers invariant subsets or the best predictive sets among them: PULSE is applicable even in situations when no invariant subsets exist.
We have proved that this estimator can also be written as a K-class estimator with data-driven

\kappa
-parameter, which lies between zero and one.
Simulation experiments show that
in various settings
with weak instruments and weak confounding,
PULSE
outperforms other estimators such as the Fuller(4) estimator.
We thus regard PULSE as an
interesting alternative for estimating causal effects
in instrumental variable settings.
It is easy to interpret and automatically provides the user feedback in case that the OLS is accepted (which may be an indication that the instruments are too weak)
or that the TSLS is outside the acceptance region (which may indicate a model misspecification).
We have applied the different estimators
to classical data sets and have seen that, indeed, K-class estimators tend to be more distributionally robust than OLS or TSLS.

There are several further directions that we consider worthwhile investigating.
This
includes better understanding of finite sample properties and for the identified setups, the study of loss functions other than MSE. It would be helpful, in particular with respect to real world applications, to understand to which extent similar principles can be applied to models allowing for a time structure of the error terms.
We believe that the simple primal form of PULSE could make it applicable for model classes that are more complex than linear models
(
Christiansen et al. 2020
, see also)
.
Our procedure can be combined with other tests and it could furthermore be interesting to find efficient optimization procedures for tests that are robust with respect to weak instruments, such as
Kleibergen’s K-statistic
(
Kleibergen 2002
)
,
for example.
In an under-identified setting, the causal parameters are not identified but the solutions obtained by optimizing predictability under invariance might be promising candidates for models that generalize well to distributional shifts.

Appendix A
Structural equation models and interventions

Structural equation models and simultaneous equation models are causal models. That is, they contain more information than the description of an observational distribution.
We first
introduce the notion of structural equation models (also called structural causal models)
and use an example to show how they can be written as in the form of
simultaneous equation models (SIM) commonly used in econometrics, see
Section

A.2
.

A.1
Structural equation models and interventions

A structural equation model (SEM)
(e.g.
Bollen 1989
, and
Pearl 2009
)
over variables
X_{1},\ldots,X_{p}
consists of

p
assignments of the form

X_{j}:=f_{j}(X_{{\mathrm{PA}({j})}},\varepsilon_{j}),\qquad j=1,\ldots,p,

where
{\mathrm{PA}({j})}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,p\}

are called the parents of
j
,
together with a distribution over
the noise variables
(\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{p})
, which is assumed to have jointly independent marginals.
The corresponding graph
over
X_{1},\ldots,X_{p}

is obtained by drawing directed edges from the variables on the right-hand side to
the variables on the left-hand side.
If the corresponding
graph is acyclic, the SEM induces a unique distribution over

(X_{1},\ldots,X_{p})
, which is often called the observational distribution.
Section
A.2
below discusses an example of linear assignments,
which also allows for a cyclic graph structure.
The framework of SEMs also models the effect of
interventions:
An intervention on variable
j
corresponds to
replacing the
j
th assignment.
For example,
replacing it by

X_{j}=4
, called a hard intervention, or, more generally, by

X_{j}=g(X_{{\widetilde{\mathrm{PA}}({j})}},\tilde{\varepsilon}_{j})

induces yet another distribution over
X
that is called an interventional distribution
and that we denote by

P^{\mathrm{do}(X_{j}=4)}
or

P^{\mathrm{do}(X_{j}=g(X_{{\widetilde{\mathrm{PA}}({j})}},\tilde{\varepsilon}_{j}))}
, respectively.
A formal introduction to SEMs,
in the general case of cyclic assignments is provided by
Bongers et al. 2016
, for example.
In an SEM,
we call all
X
variables endogenous and,
in addition, all variables
X_{j}
, for which we have

{\mathrm{PA}({j})}=\emptyset
, will be called exogenous.
A subset of variables is called exogenous relative to another subset
if it does not contain a variable
that has a parent belonging to the other set.

In the paper,
we are mostly interested in one of these equations and we denote the corresponding target variable as
Y
.
Furthermore, some of the other
X
variables may be unobserved, which we indicate by using the notation
H
(denoting a vector of variables). In linear models, hidden variables can
equivalently
be represented
as correlation in the noise variables; see e.g.
Bongers et al. 2016
, and
Hyttinen
et al. 2012
.
Finally, we let
A
denote a collection of variables that are known
to enter the system as exogenous variables, relative to
(Y,X,H)
.

A.2
Example of a linear structural equation model

Let the distribution of
(Y,X,H,A)
be generated according to the possibly cyclic
SEM,

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}Y&X^{\intercal}&H^{\intercal}\end{bmatrix}:=\begin{bmatrix}Y&X^{\intercal}&H^{\intercal}\end{bmatrix}B+A^{\intercal}M+\varepsilon^{\intercal}.

(A.1)

Here,
B
is a square matrix
with eigenvalues whose absolute value is strictly smaller than one. This implies that
I-B
is invertible ensuring that
the distribution of

(Y,X,H)
is well-defined since

(Y,X,H)

can be expressed in terms of
B,M,A
and
\varepsilon
as
(I-B^{\intercal})^{-1}(M^{\intercal}A+\varepsilon)
.
We denote the random vectors
Y\in\mathbb{R},X\in\mathbb{R}^{d},A\in\mathbb{R}^{q},H\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1+r}

by target, endogenous regressor, anchor, hidden and noise variables, respectively.
We assume that
\varepsilon\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}A
rendering the so-called anchors as exogenous variables but the coordinate components of
A
may be dependent on each other.
As above,
we assume joint independence of the noises

\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{1+d+r}
.
Let

\mathbf{Y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times 1},\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d},\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times q},\mathbf{H}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r}
and
\bm{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times(1+d+r)}
be data-matrices with
n\in\mathbb{N}
row-wise i.i.d. copies of the variables solving the system in
Equation

A.1
. Transposing the structural equations and stacking them vertically by row-wise observations, we can represent all structural equations by

[\mathbf{Y}\,\,\mathbf{X}\,\,\mathbf{H}]:=[\mathbf{Y}\,\,\mathbf{X}\,\,\mathbf{H}]B+\mathbf{A}M+\bm{\varepsilon}.

We can solve the structural equations for the endogenous variables and get the so-called structural and reduced form equations, commonly seen in econometrics,

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{Y}&\mathbf{X}&\mathbf{H}\end{bmatrix}\Gamma=\mathbf{A}M+\bm{\varepsilon}\quad\text{and}\quad\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{Y}&\mathbf{X}&\mathbf{H}\end{bmatrix}=\mathbf{A}\Pi+\bm{\varepsilon}\Gamma^{-1},

(A.2)

respectively, where
\Gamma:=I-B
and
\Pi:=M\Gamma^{-1}
.
Note that the equations in
Equation

A.2
differ from the standard representations of simultaneous equation models as we have unobserved endogenous variables
\mathbf{H}
in the system. In this setup,
identifiability of the full system parameters
\Gamma
and
M
in general breaks down due to the dependencies generated by the unobserved endogenous variables.
We now assume without loss of generality that
\Gamma
has a unity diagonal, such that the target equation of interest, corresponding to the first column of
Equation

A.2
, is given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{Y}=\mathbf{X}\gamma_{0}+\mathbf{A}\beta_{0}+\mathbf{H}\eta_{0}+\bm{\varepsilon}_{Y}=\mathbf{Z}\alpha_{0}+\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{Y},

(A.3)

where
(1,-\gamma_{0},-\eta_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{(1+d+r)}
,
\beta_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
and
\bm{\varepsilon}_{Y}
are the first columns of
\Gamma
,
M
and
\bm{\varepsilon}
respectively,
\mathbf{Z}:=[\mathbf{X}\,\,\mathbf{A}]
,
\alpha_{0}=(\gamma_{0},\beta_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{d+q}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{U}}_{Y}:=\mathbf{H}\eta_{0}+\bm{\varepsilon}_{Y}
.

The parameter of interest,
\alpha_{0}
,
can be derived directly from the corresponding entries in the matrices
B
and
M
.
It carries causal information in that, for example,
after intervening on all variables except for
Y
, that
is, considering an intervention

Z:=z
,
and

H:=h
,

Y
has the mean

z\alpha_{0}+h\eta_{0}+E\varepsilon_{1}
, see
Equation

A.1
.

In
Equation

A.3
we have represented the target variable in terms of a linear combination of the observable variables
Z=(X^{\intercal},A^{\intercal})^{\intercal}
and some unobservable noise term
\tilde{U}_{Y}
.
In contrast to
Equation

A.1
,

Equation

A.3
,
which is more commonly used in the econometrics literature,
models
the influence of the latent variables using a dependence between endogenous
variables and the noise term
\tilde{U}_{Y}
;
this equivalence is well-known and described by
Bongers et al. 2016
and
Hyttinen
et al. 2012
, for example.
The construction in
Equation

A.1
can be seen as a manifestation of Reichenbach’s common cause principle (
Reichenbach 1956
). This principle stipulates that if two random variables are dependent then either one causally influences the other or there exists a third variable which causally influences both.

Appendix B
Algorithms

In this section we present two algorithms. Algorithm 1 details a binary search procedure for the dual PULSE parameter
\lambda^{\star}_{n}(p_{\min})
and Algorithm 2
details the algorithmic construction and output messages of the PULSE estimator.

Algorithm 1

Binary.Search with precision
1/N
.

1:

input

p_{\min},N

2:

if

T_{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\text{TSLS}}^{n})\geq Q_{\chi^{2}_{q}}(1-p_{\min})

then
terminate procedure
end if

3:

\ell_{\min}\leftarrow 0
;

\ell_{\max}\leftarrow 2

4:

while

T_{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\text{K}}^{n}(\ell_{\max}))>Q_{\chi^{2}_{q}}(1-p_{\min})

do

5:

\ell_{\min}\leftarrow\ell_{\max}
;

\ell_{\max}\leftarrow\ell_{\max}^{2}

6:

end

while

7:

\Delta\leftarrow\ell_{\max}-\ell_{\min}

8:

while

\Delta>1/N

do

9:

\ell\leftarrow(\ell_{\min}+\ell_{\max})/2

10:

if

T_{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\text{K}}^{n}(\ell))>Q_{\chi^{2}_{q}}(1-p_{\min})

then

\ell_{\min}\leftarrow\ell

else

\ell_{\max}\leftarrow\ell

end if

11:

\Delta\leftarrow\ell_{\max}-\ell_{\min}

12:

end

while

13:

return(
\ell_{\max}
)

Algorithm 2

PULSE
+

1:

input

p_{\min}
, precision
1/N
,
\hat{\alpha}_{\text{ALT}}^{n}

2:

if

T_{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\text{TSLS}}^{n})\geq Q_{\chi^{2}_{q}}(1-p_{\min})

then

3:

Warning: TSLS outside interior of acceptance region.

4:

\hat{\alpha}_{\text{PULSE}+}^{n}(p_{\min})\leftarrow\hat{\alpha}_{\text{ALT}}^{n}

5:

else

6:

if

T_{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\text{OLS}}^{n})\leq Q_{\chi^{2}_{q}}(1-p_{\min})

then

7:

Warning: The OLS is accepted.

8:

\lambda^{\star}_{n}(p_{\min})\leftarrow 0

9:

else

10:

\lambda^{\star}_{n}(p_{\min})\leftarrow\text{Binary.Search}(N,p_{\min})

11:

end

if

12:

\hat{\alpha}_{\text{PULSE}+}^{n}(p_{\min})\leftarrow(\mathbf{Z}^{\intercal}(\mathbf{I}+\lambda_{n}^{\star}(p_{\min})P_{\mathbf{A}})\mathbf{Z})^{-1}\mathbf{Z}^{\intercal}(\mathbf{I}+\lambda_{n}^{\star}(p_{\min})P_{\mathbf{A}})\mathbf{Y}

13:

end

if

14:

return(
\hat{\alpha}_{\text{PULSE}+}^{n}(p_{\min})
)

Appendix C
Proofs of results in Section
2

Proof of
Proposition

2.1
:

The minimizations of
Equation

2.10
and
Equation

2.11
are unconstrained optimization problems. We know that there exists a unique solution if the problems are strictly convex. Thus, it suffices to verify the second order condition for strict convexity of the objective functions, i.e.,
D^{2}l_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\alpha;\kappa)\succ 0
. To this end, note that

Dl_{\mathrm{OLS}}^{n}(\alpha;\mathbf{Z}_{*},\mathbf{X})=2(\alpha^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}_{*}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}_{*}-\mathbf{Y}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}_{*})/n
and
Dl_{\mathrm{IV}}^{n}(\alpha;\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}_{*},\mathbf{A})=2(\alpha^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}_{*}^{\intercal}P_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{Z}_{*}-\mathbf{Y}^{\intercal}P_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{Z}_{*})/n
.
Thus, the first order derivative of the K-class regression loss function is given by the
\kappa
-weighted affine combination of these two, that is,

\displaystyle Dl_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\alpha;\kappa,\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}_{*},\mathbf{A})

\displaystyle=2n^{-1}\left((1-\kappa)\left(\alpha^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}_{*}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}_{*}-\mathbf{Y}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}_{*}\right)+\kappa\left(\alpha^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}_{*}^{\intercal}P_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{Z}_{*}-\mathbf{Y}^{\intercal}P_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{Z}_{*}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=2n^{-1}\left(\alpha^{\intercal}\left(\mathbf{Z}_{*}^{\intercal}\left((1-\kappa)\mathbf{I}+\kappa P_{\mathbf{A}}\right)\mathbf{Z}_{*}\right)-\left(\mathbf{Y}^{\intercal}\left((1-\kappa)\mathbf{I}+\kappa P_{\mathbf{A}}\right)\mathbf{Z}_{*}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=2n^{-1}\left(\alpha^{\intercal}\left(\mathbf{Z}_{*}^{\intercal}\left(\mathbf{I}-\kappa\left(\mathbf{I}-P_{\mathbf{A}}\right)\right)\mathbf{Z}_{*}\right)-\left(\mathbf{Y}^{\intercal}\left(\mathbf{I}-\kappa\left(\mathbf{I}-P_{\mathbf{A}}\right)\right)\mathbf{Z}_{*}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=2n^{-1}\left(\alpha^{\intercal}\left(\mathbf{Z}_{*}^{\intercal}\left(\mathbf{I}-\kappa P_{\mathbf{A}}^{\perp}\right)\mathbf{Z}_{*}\right)-\left(\mathbf{Y}^{\intercal}\left(\mathbf{I}-\kappa P_{\mathbf{A}}^{\perp}\right)\mathbf{Z}_{*}\right)\right),

where
P_{\mathbf{A}}^{\perp}=\mathbf{I}-P_{\mathbf{A}}
. The second order derivative is given by

\displaystyle D^{2}l_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\alpha;\kappa,\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}_{*},\mathbf{A})=2n^{-1}\mathbf{Z}_{*}^{\intercal}(I-\kappa P_{\mathbf{A}}^{\perp})\mathbf{Z}_{*},

The second derivative is
and is proportional to the matrix we need to invert in order to solve the normal equation that yields the K-class estimator. As a consequence, we have that the K-class estimator is guaranteed to exist and
be unique if the second derivative is strictly positive definite, i.e., invertible.

Let us first consider
\kappa<1
. To see that
D^{2}l_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\alpha;\kappa,\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}_{*},\mathbf{A})\succ 0
,
take any
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}+q_{1}}\setminus\{0\}
and assume that Assumption
Item

.(a)
holds. That is, we assume that
\text{rank}(\mathbf{Z}_{*}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}_{*})=\text{rank}(\mathbf{Z}_{*})=d_{1}+q_{1}
almost surely such that
z=\mathbf{Z}_{*}y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
almost surely.
Without Assumption
Item

.(a)
, choosing
y\in\ker(\mathbf{Z}_{*})\setminus\{0\}
yields a zero in the following quadratic form with positive probability. However, with this assumption (disregarding
2n^{-1}
) we get that

\displaystyle y^{\intercal}D^{2}l_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\alpha;\kappa)y

\displaystyle\propto(1-\kappa)y^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}_{*}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}_{*}y+\kappa y^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}_{*}^{\intercal}P_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{Z}_{*}y=(1-\kappa)\|z\|_{2}^{2}+\kappa\|P_{\mathbf{A}}z\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(1-\kappa)\|z\|_{2}^{2}+\kappa\|z\|_{2}^{2}=\|z\|_{2}^{2},&\text{if }\kappa\in(-\infty,0),\\
(1-\kappa)\|z\|_{2}^{2},&\text{if }\kappa\in[0,1),\end{array}\right.>0.

Here, we used that
P_{\mathbf{A}}=P_{\mathbf{A}}^{\intercal}=\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{A})^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}
is an orthogonal projection matrix, hence
P_{\mathbf{A}}=P_{\mathbf{A}}^{\intercal}P_{\mathbf{A}}
and
0\leq\|P_{\mathbf{A}}w\|_{2}^{2}\leq\|w\|_{2}^{2}
for any
w\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
.

Let us now consider the case
\kappa=1
. The quadratic form is now given by

y^{\intercal}D^{2}l_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\alpha;\kappa)y=\|P_{\mathbf{A}}z\|_{2}^{2}=y^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}_{*}^{\intercal}\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{A})^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}_{*}y.

If
\text{rank}(\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}_{*})<d_{1}+q_{1}
with positive probability, then any
y\in\ker(\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}_{*})\setminus\{0\}\not=\emptyset
yields a zero quadratic value, showing that
l_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\alpha;\kappa)
is not strictly convex with positive probability. However, if Assumption
Item

.(b)
holds, i.e., that
\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}_{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times(d_{1}+q_{1})}
satisfies
\text{rank}(\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}_{*})=d_{1}+q_{1}
almost surely, then
D^{2}l_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\alpha;\kappa)
is also guaranteed to be positive definite almost surely.

Thus, we have shown sufficient conditions for
D^{2}l_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\alpha;\kappa)
to be almost surely positive definite, ensuring strict convexity of the
l_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\alpha;\kappa)
, hence almost sure uniqueness of a global minimum. The unique global minimum is then found as a solution to the normal equation
Dl_{\text{K}}^{n}(\alpha;\kappa)=0

which is given by
\hat{\alpha}_{\text{K}}^{n}(\kappa)=(\mathbf{Z}_{*}^{\intercal}(\mathbf{I}-\kappa P_{\mathbf{A}}^{\perp})\mathbf{Z}_{*})^{-1}\mathbf{Z}_{*}^{\intercal}(\mathbf{I}-\kappa P_{\mathbf{A}}^{\perp})\mathbf{Y}
. We conclude that under the above conditions the K-class estimator
\hat{\alpha}_{\text{K}}^{n}(\kappa)
solves the unconstrained minimization problem
\argmin_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}+q_{1}}}l_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\alpha;\kappa)

almost surely.

\square

Proof of
Proposition

2.2
:

We first prove that the population estimand that minimizes the population loss function is well-defined. It suffices to show strict convexity of the population loss function. Let Assumption
Item

.(a)
hold, i.e., that
\text{Var}(Z_{*})
is positive definite, and consider
\kappa\in[0,1)
. For any
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}+q_{1}}\setminus\{0\}
we see that

\displaystyle y^{\intercal}D^{2}l_{\mathrm{K}}(\alpha;\kappa)y

\displaystyle=(1-\kappa)y^{\intercal}E(Z_{*}Z_{*}^{\intercal})y+\kappa y^{\intercal}E(Z_{*}A^{\intercal})E(AA^{\intercal})^{-1}E(AZ_{*}^{\intercal})y

\displaystyle\geq(1-\kappa)y^{\intercal}E(Z_{*}Z_{*}^{\intercal})y=(1-\kappa)\left(y^{\intercal}\text{Var}(Z_{*})y+y^{\intercal}E(Z_{*})E(Z_{*})^{\intercal}y\right)

\displaystyle\geq(1-\kappa)y^{\intercal}\text{Var}(Z_{*})y>0,

(C.1)

proving strict convexity of the K-class penalized loss function. Now let
\kappa=1
and let Assumption
Item

.(h)
and Assumption
Item

.(b)
hold, i.e.,
\text{Var}(A)
is positive definite and
E(AZ_{*}^{\intercal})
is of full column rank (which implicitly assumes we are in the just- or over-identified case). First note that by the above considerations this implies that
E(AA^{\intercal})
and its inverse
E(AA^{\intercal})^{-1}
are positive definite. For any
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}+q_{1}}\setminus\{0\}
we note that
z:=E(AZ_{*}^{\intercal})y\not=0
by injectivity of
E(AZ_{*}^{\intercal})
, and hence

y^{\intercal}D^{2}l_{\mathrm{K}}(\alpha;\kappa)y=z^{\intercal}E(AA^{\intercal})^{-1}z>0,

by the positive definiteness of
E(AA^{\intercal})^{-1}
. Proving strict convexity.

In both setups the minimization estimator of the population loss function solves the normal equation
0=Dl_{\text{K}}(\alpha;\kappa)=(1-\kappa)Dl_{\text{OLS}}(\alpha)+\kappa Dl_{\text{IV}}(\alpha)
which by rearranging the terms
yields that

\displaystyle\alpha_{\text{K}}(\kappa)=

\displaystyle\left((1-\kappa)E(Z_{*}Z_{*}^{\intercal})+\kappa E(Z_{*}A^{\intercal})E(AA^{\intercal})^{-1}E(AZ_{*}^{\intercal})\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle\cdot\left((1-\kappa)E(Z_{*}Y)+\kappa E(Z_{*}A^{\intercal})E(AA^{\intercal})^{-1}E(AY)\right).

We now prove that the estimators are asymptotically well-defined if
the population conditions of Assumption
Item

.(a)
and Assumption
Item

.(b)
hold.
For
\kappa\in[0,1)
, we know from
Proposition

2.1
that

\displaystyle P\left[\argmin_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}+q_{1}}}l_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\alpha;\kappa,\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}_{*},\mathbf{A})\textit{ is well-defined}\right]\geq P\left[\mathbf{Z}_{*}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}_{*}\textit{ is positive definite}\right],

So it suffices to show that the lower converges to one in probability. By the weak law of large numbers we have, for any
\varepsilon>0
that
P(\|\mathbf{Z}_{*}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}_{*}-E(Z_{*}Z_{*}^{\intercal})\|<\varepsilon)\to 1
.
Note that by Assumption
Item

.(a)
, i.e., that
\text{Var}(Z_{*})
is positive definite, we also have that
E(Z_{*}Z_{*}^{\intercal})
is positive definite; see
Equation

C.1
above. Note that the set of positive definite matrices
S_{+}
is an open set in the space of symmetric matrices
S
of the same dimensions. Hence, there must exist an open ball
B(E(Z_{*}Z_{*}^{\intercal}),c)\subseteq S_{+}
with center
E(Z_{*}Z_{*}^{\intercal})
and radius
c>0
, fully contained in the set of positive definite matrices. By virtue of the above convergence in probability, we have that

\displaystyle P\left[\mathbf{Z}_{*}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}_{*}\textit{ is positive definite}\right]

\displaystyle\geq P\left(\mathbf{Z}_{*}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}_{*}\in B(E(Z_{*}Z_{*}^{\intercal}),c)\right)

\displaystyle\geq P(\|\mathbf{Z}_{*}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}_{*}-E(Z_{*}Z_{*}^{\intercal})\|<c)\to 1,

proving that the estimator minimizing the K-class penalized regression function is asymptotically well-defined. In the case of
\kappa=1
the argument for asymptotic well-definedness follows by almost the same arguments. Arguing that
\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{A}
is positive definite with probability converging to one since
\text{Var}(A)
is assumed positive definite follows from the same arguments as above.
To see that
\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}_{*}
is of full column rank with probability converging to one, we use that
E(AZ_{*}^{\intercal})
is assumed full column rank. If
q=d_{1}+q_{2}
, then follows from the above arguments. Otherwise, if
q>d_{1}+q_{1}
, then we modify the above arguments using that the set of injective linear maps from
\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}+q_{1}}
to
\mathbb{R}^{q}
is an open set of all linear maps from
\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}+q_{1}}
to
\mathbb{R}^{q}
.

Finally, by the law of large numbers, Slutsky’s theorem and the continuous mapping theorem, one can easily realize that
\hat{\alpha}_{\text{K}}^{n}(\kappa)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\longrightarrow}}\alpha_{\text{K}}(\kappa)
.

\square

Proof of
Theorem

2.1
:

Let
(Y,X,H,A)
be generated by the SEM given by

\displaystyle[Y\,\,X^{\intercal}\,\,H^{\intercal}]^{\intercal}:=B[Y\,\,X^{\intercal}\,\,H^{\intercal}]^{\intercal}+MA+\varepsilon,

(C.2)

where
\varepsilon
satisfies
\varepsilon\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}A

and has jointly independent marginals
\varepsilon_{1}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}\cdots\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}\varepsilon_{d+1+r}
with finite second moment
E\|\varepsilon\|_{2}^{2}<\infty
and mean zero
E(\varepsilon)=0
. The distribution of
A
is determined independently of
Equation

C.2
and with the only requirement that
E\|A\|_{2}^{2}<\infty
. Note that we have transposed
B
and
M
for ease of notation. This implies that
(Y,X,H)
satisfies the reduced form equations given by
[Y\,\,X^{\intercal}\,\,H^{\intercal}]^{\intercal}=\Pi A+\Gamma^{-1}\varepsilon
, where
\Gamma=I-B
and
\Pi=\Gamma^{-1}M
.

Now let
X_{*}\subset X
and
A_{*}\subset A
be our candidate predictors of
Y
, regardless of which variables directly affect
Y
and let
Z_{*}=[X_{*}^{\intercal}\,\,A_{*}^{\intercal}]^{\intercal}
. By the reduced form structural equations we derive the marginal reduced forms as

\displaystyle Y=\Pi_{Y}A+\Gamma^{-1}_{Y}\varepsilon\quad\text{and}\quad X_{*}=\Pi_{X_{*}}A+\Gamma^{-1}_{X_{*}}\varepsilon,

(C.3)

where
\Pi_{Y},\Pi_{X_{*}},\Gamma^{-1}_{Y},\Gamma^{-1}_{X_{*}}
are the relevant sub-matrices of rows from
\Pi
and
\Gamma^{-1}
. Furthermore, let
(Y^{v},X^{v},H^{v})

be generated as a solution to the SEM of
Equation

C.2
under the intervention
\text{do}(A:=v)
, where
v\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},P)
is any fixed stochastic element uncorrelated with
\varepsilon
. Under the intervention and by similar manipulations as above, we arrive at the following marginal reduced forms
Y^{v}=\Pi_{Y}v+\Gamma^{-1}_{Y}\varepsilon
and
X_{*}^{v}=\Pi_{X_{*}}v+\Gamma^{-1}_{X_{*}}\varepsilon.

For a fixed
\gamma
and
\beta
, with
A_{-*}
being
A\setminus A_{*}
, we have that

\displaystyle Y-\gamma^{\intercal}X_{*}-\beta^{\intercal}A_{*}

\displaystyle=(\Pi_{Y}-\gamma^{\intercal}\Pi_{X_{*}})A+(\Gamma_{Y}^{-1}-\Gamma_{X_{*}}^{-1})\varepsilon-\beta^{\intercal}A_{*}

\displaystyle=(\delta_{1}^{\intercal}-\beta^{\intercal})A_{*}+\delta_{2}^{\intercal}A_{-*}+w^{\intercal}\varepsilon=\xi^{\intercal}A+w^{\intercal}\varepsilon,

where
\delta_{1}
,
\delta_{2}
are such that

(\Pi_{Y}-\gamma^{\intercal}\Pi_{X_{*}})A=\delta_{1}^{\intercal}A_{*}+\delta_{2}^{\intercal}A_{-*}
,
\xi
is such that

\xi^{\intercal}A=(\delta_{1}^{\intercal}-\beta^{\intercal})A_{*}+\delta_{2}^{\intercal}A_{-*}
and
w^{\intercal}:=(\Gamma_{Y}^{-1}-\Gamma_{X_{*}}^{-1})
. Similar manipulations yield that
the regression residuals
under the intervention are given by

Y^{v}-\gamma^{\intercal}X_{*}^{v}-\beta^{\intercal}v_{*}=\xi^{\intercal}v+w^{\intercal}\varepsilon.

Since
A\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}\varepsilon
and
\varepsilon
has mean zero, we have that

\displaystyle E(Y-\gamma^{\intercal}X_{*}-\beta^{\intercal}A_{*}|A)

\displaystyle=\xi^{\intercal}A+w^{\intercal}E(\varepsilon)=\xi^{\intercal}A,

(C.4)

\displaystyle Y-\gamma^{\intercal}X_{*}-\beta^{\intercal}A_{*}-E(Y-\gamma^{\intercal}X_{*}-\beta^{\intercal}A_{*}|A)

\displaystyle=w^{\intercal}\varepsilon.

(C.5)

By construction
E(v\varepsilon^{\intercal})=0
, so

\displaystyle E^{\text{do}(A:=v)}\left[\left(Y-\gamma^{\intercal}X_{*}-\beta^{\intercal}A_{*}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=E\left[\left(\xi^{\intercal}v+w^{\intercal}\varepsilon\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=E\left[\left(\xi^{\intercal}v\right)^{2}\right]+E\left[\left(w^{\intercal}\varepsilon\right)^{2}\right]+\xi^{\intercal}E(v\varepsilon^{\intercal})w

\displaystyle=E\left[\left(\xi^{\intercal}v\right)^{2}\right]+E\left[\left(w^{\intercal}\varepsilon\right)^{2}\right].

(C.6)

We investigate the terms of
Equation

C.6
and note by
Equation

C.5
that

\displaystyle E\left[\left(w^{\intercal}\varepsilon\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=E\left[\left(Y-\gamma^{\intercal}X_{*}-\beta^{\intercal}A_{*}-E(Y-\gamma^{\intercal}X_{*}-\beta^{\intercal}A_{*}|A)\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=E\left[\left(Y-\gamma^{\intercal}X_{*}-\beta^{\intercal}A_{*}\right)^{2}\right]+E\left[E\left(Y-\gamma^{\intercal}X_{*}-\beta^{\intercal}A_{*}|A\right)^{2}\right]

(C.7)

\displaystyle\qquad-2E\left[\left(Y-\gamma^{\intercal}X_{*}-\beta^{\intercal}A_{*}\right)E(Y-\gamma^{\intercal}X_{*}-\beta^{\intercal}A_{*}|A)\right].

In
Equation

C.4
we established that
E(Y-\gamma^{\intercal}X_{*}-\beta^{\intercal}A_{*}|A)
is a linear function of
A
, so it must hold that

\displaystyle E(Y-\gamma^{\intercal}X_{*}-\beta^{\intercal}A_{*}|A)

\displaystyle=\argmin_{Z\in\sigma(A)}\|Y-\gamma^{\intercal}X_{*}-\beta^{\intercal}A_{*}-Z\|_{L^{2}(P)}^{2}

\displaystyle=A^{\intercal}\argmin_{c\in\mathbb{R}^{q}}\|Y-\gamma^{\intercal}X_{*}-\beta^{\intercal}A_{*}-A^{\intercal}c\|_{L^{2}(P)}^{2}

\displaystyle=A^{\intercal}E(AA^{\intercal})^{-1}E\left[A\left(Y-\gamma^{\intercal}X_{*}-\beta^{\intercal}A_{*}\right)\right],

almost surely. In the first equality we used that the conditional expectation is the best predictor under the
L^{2}(P)
-norm and in the third equality we used that the minimizer is given by the population ordinary least square estimate.
An immediate consequence of this is that the second term of
Equation

C.7
equals

\displaystyle E\left[E\left(Y-\gamma^{\intercal}X_{*}-\beta^{\intercal}A_{*}|A\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=E[(Y-\gamma^{\intercal}X_{*}-\beta^{\intercal}A_{*})A^{\intercal}]E(AA^{\intercal})^{-1}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\cdot E[A(Y-\gamma^{\intercal}X_{*}-\beta^{\intercal}A_{*})],

which is seen to be of the same form of the third term in
Equation

C.7
,

\displaystyle E\left[\left(Y-\gamma^{\intercal}X_{*}-\beta^{\intercal}A_{*}\right)E(Y-\gamma^{\intercal}X_{*}-\beta^{\intercal}A_{*}|A)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E\left[\left(Y-\gamma^{\intercal}X_{*}-\beta^{\intercal}A_{*}\right)A^{\intercal}\right]E(AA^{\intercal})^{-1}E\left[A(Y-\gamma^{\intercal}X_{*}-\beta^{\intercal}A_{*})\right].

Thus, we conclude that the second term of
Equation

C.6
is given by

\displaystyle E\left[\left(w^{\intercal}\varepsilon\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=E\left[\left(Y-\gamma^{\intercal}X_{*}-\beta^{\intercal}A_{*}\right)^{2}\right]-E\left[E\left(Y-\gamma^{\intercal}X_{*}-\beta^{\intercal}A_{*}|A\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=l_{\mathrm{OLS}}(\alpha;Y,Z_{*})-l_{\mathrm{IV}}(\alpha;Y,Z_{*},A).

Taking the supremum over all
v\in C(\kappa)
of the first term of
Equation

C.6
we obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{v\in C(\kappa)}E\left[\left(\xi^{\intercal}v\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\sup_{v\in C(\kappa)}\xi^{\intercal}E\left[vv^{\intercal}\right]\xi=\frac{1}{1-\kappa}\xi^{\intercal}E\left[AA^{\intercal}\right]\xi=\frac{1}{1-\kappa}E\left[\left(\xi^{\intercal}A\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-\kappa}E\left[E\left(Y-\gamma^{\intercal}X_{*}-\beta^{\intercal}A_{*}|A\right)^{2}\right]=\frac{1}{1-\kappa}l_{\mathrm{IV}}(\alpha;Y,Z_{*},A),

where the
second last equation follows from

Equation

C.4

and the
second equation follows from the following argument. For any
v\in C(\kappa)
we have that
E(vv^{\intercal})\preceq\frac{1}{1-\kappa}E(AA^{\intercal})
, that is, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
it holds that
\frac{1}{1-\kappa}x^{\intercal}E(AA^{\intercal})x\geq x^{\intercal}E(vv^{\intercal})x
, which implies that the upper bound is attained for any
v
such that
E(vv^{\intercal})=\frac{1}{1-\kappa}E(AA^{\intercal})
.
Thus, we have that

\displaystyle\sup_{v\in C(\kappa)}E^{\mathrm{do}(A:=v)}\left[(Y-\gamma^{\intercal}X_{*}-\beta^{\intercal}A_{*})^{2}\right]=

\displaystyle\sup_{v\in C(\kappa)}E\left[\left(\xi^{\intercal}v\right)^{2}\right]+E\left[\left(w^{\intercal}\varepsilon\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle l_{\mathrm{OLS}}(\alpha;Y,Z_{*})+\frac{\kappa}{1-\kappa}l_{\mathrm{IV}}(\alpha;Y,Z_{*},A).

By the representation in
Equation

2.13
it therefore follows that the population K-class estimate with parameter
\kappa\not=1
is given as the estimate that minimizes the worst case mean squared prediction error over all interventions contained in
C(\kappa)
, that is,

\displaystyle\alpha_{\mathrm{K}}(\kappa;Z_{*},A)

\displaystyle=\argmin_{\gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{q_{1}}}\sup_{v\in C(\kappa)}E^{\mathrm{do}(A:=v)}\left[(Y-\gamma^{\intercal}X_{*}-\beta^{\intercal}A_{*})^{2}\right].

\square

Appendix D
Proofs of selected results in Section
3

Corollary D.1

(K-class estimators differ)
.

Let
3.9
and
3.6
hold.
If
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\geq 0
with
\lambda_{1}\not=\lambda_{2}
, then
\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{1})\not=\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{2})
.

Proof:

Let
3.6
and
3.9
hold.
\hat{\alpha}_{\text{K}}^{n}(\lambda)
is well-defined for all
\lambda\geq 0
by
Proposition

2.1
. Let
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\geq 0

with
\lambda_{1}\not=\lambda_{2}
and note that the orthogonality condition derived in the proof of
Lemma

3.3
also applies here. That is,
\langle\mathbf{Y}-\mathbf{Z}\hat{\alpha}_{\text{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{i}),(\mathbf{I}+\lambda_{i}P_{\mathbf{A}})z\rangle=0,
for all
z\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{Z})
and
i=1,2
. Assume for contradiction that
\hat{\alpha}_{\text{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{1})=\hat{\alpha}_{\text{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{2})
. This implies that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\langle\mathbf{Y}-\mathbf{Z}\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{1}),(\mathbf{I}+\lambda_{1}P_{\mathbf{A}})z-(\mathbf{I}+\lambda_{2}P_{\mathbf{A}})z\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathbf{Y}-\mathbf{Z}\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{1}),(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})P_{\mathbf{A}}z\rangle=(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})\langle\mathbf{Y}-\mathbf{Z}\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{1}),P_{\mathbf{A}}z\rangle,

for any
z\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{Z})
. Thus, by symmetry and idempotency of
P_{\mathbf{A}}
we have that for all
z\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{Z})
,

\langle P_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{Y}-P_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{Z}\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{1}),P_{\mathbf{A}}z\rangle=\langle\mathbf{Y}-\mathbf{Z}\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{1}),P_{\mathbf{A}}z\rangle=0.

That is,
P_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{Z}\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{1})
is the orthogonal projection of
P_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{Y}
onto
\mathcal{R}(P_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{Z})
.
This is equivalent with saying that
\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{1})\in\mathcal{M}_{\text{IV}}
as the space of minimizers of
l_{\text{IV}}^{n}
are exactly the coefficients in
\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}+q_{1}}
which mapped through
P_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{Z}
yields this orthogonal projection. See the proof of
Lemma

3.3
for further elaboration on this equivalence.
This is a contradiction to
3.9
, hence
\hat{\alpha}_{\text{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{1})\not=\hat{\alpha}_{\text{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{2})
.

\square

Lemma D.8

(Monotonicity of the losses and the test statistic)
.

,

When Assumption
Item

.(a)
holds the maps
[0,\infty)\ni\lambda\mapsto l_{\mathrm{OLS}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda))
and
[0,\infty)\ni\lambda\mapsto l_{\mathrm{IV}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda))

are monotonically increasing and monotonically decreasing, respectively. Consequently, if
3.7
holds, we have that the map

[0,\infty)\ni\lambda\longmapsto T_{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda))

is monotonically decreasing. Furthermore, if
3.9
also holds, these monotonicity statements can be strengthened to strictly decreasing and strictly increasing.

Proof:

Let Assumption
Item

.(a)
hold, such that
\hat{\alpha}_{\text{K}}^{n}(\lambda)
is well-defined for all
\lambda\geq 0
; see
Proposition

2.1
.
Let
\lambda_{2}>\lambda_{1}\geq 0
and note that

\displaystyle l_{\mathrm{OLS}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{1}))

\displaystyle+\lambda_{1}l_{\mathrm{IV}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{1}))\leq l_{\mathrm{OLS}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{2}))+\lambda_{1}l_{\mathrm{IV}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{2}))

\displaystyle=l_{\mathrm{OLS}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{2}))+\lambda_{2}l_{\mathrm{IV}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{2}))+(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})l_{\mathrm{IV}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{2}))

\displaystyle\leq l_{\mathrm{OLS}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{1}))+\lambda_{2}l_{\mathrm{IV}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{1}))+(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})l_{\mathrm{IV}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{2})),

where we used that
\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda)
minimizes the expressions with penalty factor
\lambda
. Thus,

(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})l_{\mathrm{IV}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{1}))\leq(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})l_{\mathrm{IV}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{2})),

which is equivalent with

l_{\mathrm{IV}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{1}))\geq l_{\mathrm{IV}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{2})),

proving that
\lambda\mapsto l_{\mathrm{IV}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda))
is monotonically decreasing.

If
\lambda_{2}>\lambda_{1}=0
, then we note that

l_{\mathrm{OLS}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{1}))=\min_{\alpha}\{l_{\mathrm{OLS}}^{n}(\alpha)\}\leq l_{\mathrm{OLS}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{2})).

For any
\lambda>0
,

\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda)=\argmin_{\alpha}\{l_{\mathrm{OLS}}^{n}(\alpha)+\lambda l_{\mathrm{IV}}^{n}(\alpha)\}=\argmin_{\alpha}\{\lambda^{-1}l_{\mathrm{OLS}}^{n}(\alpha)+l_{\mathrm{IV}}^{n}(\alpha)\}.

Thus, if
\lambda_{2}>\lambda_{1}>0,
we have that

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}^{-1}l_{\mathrm{OLS}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{1}))+

\displaystyle l_{\mathrm{IV}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{1}))\leq\lambda_{1}^{-1}l_{\mathrm{OLS}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{2}))+l_{\mathrm{IV}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{2}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda_{2}^{-1}l_{\mathrm{OLS}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{2}))+l_{\mathrm{IV}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{2}))+(\lambda_{1}^{-1}-\lambda_{2}^{-1})l_{\mathrm{OLS}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{2}))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\lambda_{2}^{-1}l_{\mathrm{OLS}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{1}))+l_{\mathrm{IV}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{1}))+(\lambda_{1}^{-1}-\lambda_{2}^{-1})l_{\mathrm{OLS}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{2})),

hence
l_{\mathrm{OLS}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{1}))\leq l_{\mathrm{OLS}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{2})),

so
\lambda\mapsto l_{\mathrm{OLS}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda))
is monotonically increasing.

When
3.7
holds, the map

\lambda\mapsto T_{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda))=n\frac{l_{\text{IV}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda))}{l_{\mathrm{OLS}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda))},

is well-defined and monotonically decreasing, as it is given by a positive, monotonically decreasing function over a strictly positive and monotonically increasing function.

Furthermore, when
3.9
holds,

Corollary

3.1
yields that for
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\geq 0
with
\lambda_{1}\not=\lambda_{2}
it holds that
\hat{\alpha}_{\text{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{1})\not=\hat{\alpha}_{\text{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{2})
.
As a consequence, the above inequalities become strict,
since
otherwise (Dual.
\lambda.n
) has two distinct solutions which contradicts

Proposition

2.1
. Replacing the above inequalities with strict inequalities yields that the functions are strictly increasing and decreasing, respectively.

\square

Lemma D.9
.

Let
p_{\min}\in(0,1)
and let Assumption
Item

.(a)
and
3.7
hold. If
\lambda_{n}^{\star}(p_{\min})<\infty
, it holds that

\displaystyle T_{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{n}^{\star}(p_{\min})))\leq Q_{\chi^{2}_{q}}(1-p_{\min}).

(D.1)

If the ordinary least square estimator satisfies

T_{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{OLS}}^{n})<Q_{\chi^{2}_{q}}(1-p_{\min})
, then
Equation

D.1
holds with strict inequality, otherwise it holds with equality.

Proof:

Let
p_{\min}\in(0,1)
and let let Assumption
Item

.(a)
and
3.7
hold, such that
\hat{\alpha}_{\text{K}}^{n}(\lambda)
for all
\lambda\geq 0
and
T_{n}(\alpha)
for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}+q_{1}}
are well-defined, by
Proposition

2.1
.

Assume that
\lambda_{n}^{\star}(p_{\min})<\infty
, so we know that
T_{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\text{K}}^{n}(\lambda))\leq Q_{\chi^{2}_{q}}(1-p)
for all
\lambda>\lambda_{n}^{\star}(p_{\min})
by the monotonicity of
Lemma

3.6
.
Thus, the first statement follows
if we can show that
\lambda\mapsto T_{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\text{K}}^{n}(\lambda))
is a continuous function. Since
\alpha\mapsto T_{n}(\alpha)
is continuous it suffices to show that
[0,\infty)\ni\lambda\mapsto\hat{\alpha}_{\text{K}}^{n}(\lambda)
is continuous. Recall that
\hat{\alpha}_{\text{K}}^{n}(\lambda)=(\mathbf{Z}^{\intercal}(\mathbf{I}+\lambda P_{\mathbf{A}})\mathbf{Z})^{-1}\mathbf{Z}^{\intercal}(\mathbf{I}+\lambda P_{\mathbf{A}})\mathbf{Y},
for any
\lambda\geq 0
. Note that the functions

\text{Inv}:S_{++}^{d_{1}+q_{1}}\to S_{++}^{d_{1}+q_{1}}
given by
\mathbf{M}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Inv}}}{{\mapsto}}\mathbf{M}^{-1}
,
\lambda\mapsto\mathbf{Z}^{\intercal}(\mathbf{I}+\lambda P_{\mathbf{A}})\mathbf{Z}
,
\lambda\mapsto\mathbf{Z}^{\intercal}(\mathbf{I}+\lambda P_{\mathbf{A}})\mathbf{Y}
and
(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{C})\mapsto\mathbf{B}\mathbf{C}

are all continuous maps, where
S_{++}^{d_{1}+q_{1}}
is the set of all positive definite
(d_{1}+q_{1})\times(d_{1}+q_{1})
matrices. We have that
\lambda\mapsto\hat{\alpha}_{\text{K}}^{n}(\lambda)
is a composition of these continuous maps, hence it itself is continuous. This proves the first statement.

In the case that OLS is strictly feasible in the PULSE problem,
T_{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\text{OLS}}^{n})<Q_{\chi^{2}_{q}}(1-p_{\min})
, we have that

\lambda^{\star}(p_{\min})=\inf\left\{\lambda\geq 0:T_{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\text{K}}^{n}(\lambda))\leq Q_{\chi^{2}_{q}}(1-p_{\min})\right\}=0,

since
\hat{\alpha}_{\text{K}}^{n}(0)=\hat{\alpha}_{\text{OLS}}^{n}
, hence

T_{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{n}^{\star}(p_{\min})))=T_{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(0))=T_{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\text{OLS}}^{n})<Q_{\chi^{2}_{q}}(1-p_{\min}).

Similar arguments show that, if the OLS is just-feasible in the PULSE problem,
T_{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\text{OLS}}^{n})=Q_{\chi^{2}_{q}}(1-p_{\min})
, then
T_{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{n}^{\star}(p_{\min})))=Q_{\chi^{2}_{q}}(1-p_{\min})
.

In the case that the OLS estimator is infeasible in the PULSE problem,
Q_{\chi^{2}_{q}}(1-p_{\min})<T_{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\text{OLS}}^{n})
, continuity and monotonicity of
\lambda\mapsto T_{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\text{K}}^{n}(\lambda))

entail it must hold that
T_{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\text{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{n}^{\star}(p_{\min})))=Q_{\chi^{2}_{q}}(1-p_{\min})
,
as otherwise

T_{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\text{K}}^{n}(\lambda_{n}^{\star}(p_{\min})))<Q_{\chi^{2}_{q}}(1-p_{\min})<T_{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\text{K}}^{n}(0)),

implying that there exists
\tilde{\lambda}<\lambda_{n}^{\star}(p_{\min})
such that

T_{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\text{K}}^{n}(\tilde{\lambda}))\leq Q_{\chi^{2}_{q}}(1-p_{\min})
, contradicting
\lambda_{n}^{\star}(p_{\min})=\inf\{\lambda\geq 0:T_{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{K}}^{n}(\lambda))\leq Q_{\chi^{2}_{q}}(1-p_{\min})\}
.

\square

Appendix E
Proofs of remaining results in Section
3

Proof of
Lemma

3.1
:

We want to show an asymptotic guarantee that type I errors (rejecting a true hypothesis) occur with probability
p
. That is, if
\mathcal{H}_{0}(\alpha)
is true, then
P(T_{n}^{c}(\alpha)>Q_{\chi^{2}_{q}}(1-p))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}p
. Furthermore, we want to show that for any fixed alternative, the probability of type II errors (failure to reject) converges to zero. That is, if
P
is such that
\mathcal{H}_{0}(\alpha)
is false, then
P(T_{n}^{c}(\alpha)\leq Q_{\chi^{2}_{q}}(1-p))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}0
.

Fix any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}+q_{1}}
. It suffices to show that under the null-hypothesis

T_{n}^{c}(\alpha)
is asymptotically Chi-squared distributed with
q
degrees of freedom and that
T_{n}^{c}(\alpha)
tends to infinity under any fixed alternative. Without loss of generality assume that
c(n)=n
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and recall that

T_{n}^{c}(n)=T_{n}(\alpha)=n\frac{l_{\text{IV}}^{n}(\alpha)}{l_{\text{OLS}}^{n}(\alpha)}=n\frac{\|P_{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbf{Y}-\mathbf{Z}\alpha)\|_{2}^{2}}{\|\mathbf{Y}-\mathbf{Z}\alpha\|_{2}^{2}}.

By the idempotency of
P_{\mathbf{A}}
the
numerator can be rewritten as

\|P_{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbf{Y}-\mathbf{Z}\alpha)\|_{2}^{2}=\|(\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{A})^{-1/2}\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{R}(\alpha)\|_{2}^{2},

while the denominator takes the form
\|\mathbf{R}(\alpha)\|_{2}^{2}
. Here,
\mathbf{R}(\alpha):=\mathbf{Y}-\mathbf{Z}\alpha
and
R(\alpha):=Y-Z^{\intercal}\alpha
denotes the empirical and population regression residuals, respectively.
3.7
ensures that
T_{n}
is well-defined on the entire domain of
\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}+q_{1}}
as the denominator is never zero. Furthermore, note that both
R(\alpha)
for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}+q_{1}}
and
A_{i}
for any
i=1,...,q
have finite second moments
by virtue of Assumption
Item

.(f)
.

Assume that the null hypothesis of zero correlation between the components of

A
and the regression residuals
R(\alpha)
holds. First we show that the null hypothesis, under the stated assumptions, implies independence between the exogenous variables
A
and the regression residuals
R(\alpha)
.
It holds that
E(AR(\alpha))=E(A)E(R(\alpha))=0
by Assumption
Item

.(b)
, the mean zero assumption of
A
. Assumption
Item

.(a)
, i.e.,
A\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}U_{Y}
, yields that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=E(AR(\alpha))=E(AZ^{\intercal})(\alpha_{0}-\alpha)+E(AU_{Y})=E(AZ^{\intercal})(\alpha_{0}-\alpha),

(E.1)

proving that
\alpha-\alpha_{0}=w

for some
w\in\text{kern}(E(AZ^{\intercal}))
. Recall that
the marginal structural equation of
Equation

C.3
states that
X_{*}=\Pi_{X_{*}}A+\Gamma^{-1}_{X_{*}}\varepsilon
. Thus,
Z
has
the following representation

\displaystyle Z=\begin{bmatrix}X_{*}\\
A_{*}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\Pi_{X_{*}}A+\Gamma^{-1}_{X_{*}}\varepsilon\\
A_{*}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\Pi_{X_{*}}^{(*)}&\Pi_{X_{*}}^{(-*)}\\
I&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}A_{*}\\
A_{-*}\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}\Gamma_{X_{*}}^{-1}\\
0\end{bmatrix}\varepsilon=:\Lambda A+\Psi\varepsilon,

where
\Pi_{X_{*}}=[\Pi_{X_{*}}^{(*)}\,\,\Pi_{X_{*}}^{(-*)}]\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}\times(q_{1}+q_{2})}
and
\Lambda
,
\Psi
are the
conformable block-matrices. Since
A\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}\varepsilon
by Assumption
Item

.(d)
we have that
E(A\varepsilon^{\intercal})=0
, hence

0=E(AZ^{\intercal})w=E(AA^{\intercal})\Lambda^{\intercal}w+E(A\varepsilon^{\intercal})\Psi^{\intercal}w=E(AA^{\intercal})\Lambda^{\intercal}w.

This proves that
\Lambda^{\intercal}w=0
as
E(AA^{\intercal})
is of full rank by Assumption
Item

.(h)
. Hence,

\displaystyle R(\alpha)

\displaystyle=Y-Z^{\intercal}\alpha=Z^{\intercal}(\alpha_{0}-\alpha)+U_{Y}=Z^{\intercal}w+U_{Y}

\displaystyle=A^{\intercal}\Lambda^{\intercal}w+\varepsilon^{\intercal}\Psi^{\intercal}w+U_{Y}=\varepsilon^{\intercal}\Psi^{\intercal}w+U_{Y}.

Furthermore,
U_{Y}=\alpha_{0,-*}^{\intercal}Z_{-*}+\eta^{\intercal}_{0}H+\varepsilon_{Y}
can be written as a linear function of
A
plus a linear function of
\varepsilon
. To realize this, simply express
Z_{-*}
and
H
by their marginal reduced form structural equations. Hence, the assumptions that
A\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}U_{Y}
must entail that
A
vanishes from the expression of
U_{Y}
. As a consequence we have that
R(\alpha)
is a linear function only of
\varepsilon
, from which the assumption that
A\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}\varepsilon
yields that
A\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}R(\alpha)
. That is, the null hypothesis of zero correlation implies independence in the linear structural equation model,
under the given assumptions. Thus,
E\|AR(\alpha)\|_{2}^{2}=E\|A\|_{2}^{2}E\|R(\alpha)\|_{2}^{2}<\infty
, so the covariance matrix of
AR(\alpha)
is well-defined.

By the established independence and
Equation

E.1
, the covariance matrix of
AR(\alpha)
has the following representation

\mathrm{Cov}(AR(\alpha))=E(AA^{\intercal})E(R(\alpha)^{2})\succ 0.

The positive definiteness follows from the facts that
E(AA^{\intercal})\succ 0
and
E(R(\alpha)^{2})>0
for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}+q_{1}}
.
E(AA^{\intercal})\succ 0
follows by Assumption
Item

.(h)
and
E(R(\alpha)^{2})>0
for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}+q_{1}}
follows by Assumption
Item

.(b)
, Assumption
Item

.(c)
and
3.5
; non-degeneracy and mutual independence of the marginal noise variables in
\varepsilon
. To see this, expand
R(\alpha)
in terms of the marginal reduced form structural equations of
Y
and
Z
and use that
(I-B^{\intercal})
is invertible to see that
\varepsilon
does not vanish in the expression
R(\alpha)
. The multi-dimensional Central Limit Theorem yields that

\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{R}(\alpha)=\sqrt{n}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\begin{pmatrix}A_{i,1}R(\alpha)_{i}\\
\vdots\\
A_{i,q}R(\alpha)_{i}\end{pmatrix}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Cov}(AR(\alpha))).

Furthermore, note that regardless of whether or not the null-hypothesis is true, we have that

\sqrt{n}(\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{A})^{-1/2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\longrightarrow}}E(AA^{\intercal})^{-1/2},

and

\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\|\mathbf{R}(\alpha)\|_{2}=\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}R(\alpha)_{i}^{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\longrightarrow}}\sqrt{E(R(\alpha)^{2})}>0,

by the law of large numbers and the continuity of the matrix square root operation on the cone of symmetric positive-definite matrices. We can represent the test-statistic as
T_{n}(\alpha):=\|\sqrt{n}W_{n}(\alpha)\|_{2}^{2}
with

W_{n}(\alpha):=(\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{A})^{-1/2}\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{R}(\alpha)/\|\mathbf{R}(\alpha)\|_{2},

and have that
\sqrt{n}W_{n}(\alpha)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\longrightarrow}}W\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,I\right)
,
by Slutsky’s theorem and linear transformation rules of multivariate normal distributions. Hence, the continuous mapping theorem yields that

T_{n}(\alpha)=\left\|\sqrt{n}W_{n}(\alpha)\right\|_{2}^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\|W\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{q}W_{i}^{2}\sim\chi^{2}_{q},

where
\chi^{2}_{q}
is the Chi-squared distribution with
q
degrees of freedom, since
W_{1}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}\cdots\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}W_{q}
. This proves that the test-statistic
T_{n}
has the correct asymptotic distribution under the null-hypothesis.

Now fix a distribution
P
, for which the null hypothesis of simultaneous zero correlation between the components of
A
and the residuals
R(\alpha)
does not hold. That is, there exists an
j\in\{1,...,q\}
such that
E(A_{j}R(\alpha))\not=E(A_{j})E(R(\alpha))=0
. Note that

\displaystyle\left\|n^{-1/2}(\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{A})^{1/2}\right\|_{\text{op}}^{2}T_{n}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\left\|n^{-1/2}(\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{A})^{1/2}\right\|_{\text{op}}^{2}\left\|\frac{\sqrt{n}(\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{A})^{-1/2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{R}(\alpha)}{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\|\mathbf{R}(\alpha)\|_{2}}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\left\|\frac{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{R}(\alpha)}{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\|\mathbf{R}(\alpha)\|_{2}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\geq\left|\frac{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\mathbf{A}_{j}^{\intercal}\mathbf{R}(\alpha)}{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\|\mathbf{R}(\alpha)\|_{2}}\right|^{2}=n\left|\frac{\frac{1}{n}\mathbf{A}_{j}^{\intercal}\mathbf{R}(\alpha)}{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\|\mathbf{R}(\alpha)\|_{2}}\right|^{2},

where
\mathbf{A}_{j}^{\intercal}:=(\mathbf{A}_{j})^{\intercal}
and
\mathbf{A}_{j}
is the
j
’th column
of
\mathbf{A}
corresponding to the i.i.d. vector consisting of
n
copies of the
j
’th exogenous variable
A_{j}
and
\|\cdot\|_{\text{op}}
is the operator norm.
The lower bound diverges to infinity in probability
as the latter factor tends to
|E(A_{j}R(\alpha))/\sqrt{E(R(\alpha)^{2})}|^{2}>0
in probability by the law of large numbers and Slutsky’s theorem. Hence, it holds that
T_{n}(\alpha)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle P}}{{\longrightarrow}}\infty,

as

\left\|n^{-1/2}(\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{A})^{1/2}\right\|_{\text{op}}\to\left\|E(AA^{\intercal})^{1/2}\right\|_{\text{op}}\in(0,\infty).

This concludes the proof.

\square

Proof of
Lemma

3.2
:

Let
3.6
hold, i.e., that
\mathbf{Z}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}
and
\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}
are of full rank.
That
\alpha\mapsto l_{\mathrm{IV}}^{n}(\alpha;\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z},\mathbf{A})
is a convex function and
\alpha\mapsto l_{\text{OLS}}^{n}(\alpha)
is a strictly convex function can be seen from the quadratic forms of their second derivatives, i.e.,

y^{\intercal}D^{2}l_{\text{IV}}^{n}(\alpha)y=2n^{-1}y^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}^{\intercal}\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{A})^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}y=2n^{-1}\|(\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{A})^{-1/2}\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}y\|_{2}^{2}\geq 0,

and

y^{\intercal}D^{2}l_{\text{OLS}}^{n}(\alpha)y=2n^{-1}y^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}y=2n^{-1}\|\mathbf{Z}y\|_{2}^{2}>0,

for any
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}+q_{1}}\setminus\{0\}
. Here, we also used that
\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{A}
is of full rank by Assumption
Item

.(i)
and that
\mathbf{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times(d_{1}+q_{1})}
is an injective linear transformation as
d_{1}+q_{1}=\text{rank}(\mathbf{Z}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z})=\text{rank}(\mathbf{Z})
.

Suppose that there exists two optimal solutions
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
to the (Primal
.t.n
) problem. By the convexity of the feasibility set any convex combination is also feasible. However,

l_{\mathrm{OLS}}^{n}\left(\alpha_{1}/2+\alpha_{2}/2\right)<l_{\mathrm{OLS}}^{n}(\alpha_{1})/2+l_{\mathrm{OLS}}^{n}(\alpha_{2})/2=l_{\mathrm{OLS}}^{n}(\alpha_{1}),

since
l_{\mathrm{OLS}}^{n}(\alpha_{1})=l_{\mathrm{OLS}}^{n}(\alpha_{2})
. This means that
\alpha_{1}/2+\alpha_{2}/2
has a strictly better objective value than the optimal point
\alpha_{1}
, which is a contradiction.
Hence, there cannot exist multiple solutions to the optimization problem (Primal
.t.n
).

Regarding the claim of solvability, note that
\mathbf{Z}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}
is positive definite and as a consequence the smallest eigenvalue
\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{Z}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z})
is strictly positive. Thus, using the lower bound of the symmetric quadratic form
\alpha^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}\alpha\geq\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{Z}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z})\|\alpha\|_{2}^{2}
, we get that

\displaystyle l_{\text{OLS}}^{n}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Y}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Y}+\alpha^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}\alpha-2\mathbf{Y}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}\alpha\geq\mathbf{Y}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Y}+\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{Z}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z})\|\alpha\|_{2}^{2}-2|\mathbf{Y}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}\alpha|

\displaystyle\geq\mathbf{Y}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Y}+\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{Z}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z})\|\alpha\|_{2}^{2}-2\|\mathbf{Y}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}\|_{\text{op}}\|\alpha\|_{2}\to\infty,

(E.2)

as
\|\alpha\|_{2}\to\infty
, where we used that for the linear operator
\mathbf{Y}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}+q_{1}}\to\mathbb{R}
the operator
norm is given by
\|\mathbf{Y}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}\|_{\text{op}}:=\inf\{c\geq 0:|\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{Z}v|\leq c\|v\|_{2},\forall v\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}+q_{1}}\}
, obviously satisfying
|\mathbf{Y}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}v|\leq\|\mathbf{Y}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}\|_{\text{op}}\|v\|_{2}
for any
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}+q_{1}}
.

Now assume that
t>\inf_{\alpha}l_{\text{IV}}^{n}(\alpha)
.
This implies that there exists at least one point
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}+q_{1}}
such that
l_{\text{IV}}^{n}(\tilde{\alpha})\leq t
, hence
we only need to consider points
\alpha
such that
l_{\text{OLS}}^{n}(\alpha)\leq l_{\text{OLS}}^{n}(\tilde{\alpha})

as possible solutions of the optimization problem. By the considerations
in
Equation

E.2

above, there exists

c\geq 0
such that is suffices to search over the closed ball
\overline{B(0,c)}
.
Indeed,
for a sufficiently large
c\geq 0
we know that
\alpha\not\in\overline{B(0,c)}
implies that
l_{\text{OLS}}^{n}(\alpha)>l_{\text{OLS}}^{n}(\tilde{\alpha})
by
Equation

E.2
. Furthermore, as the inequality constraint function
\alpha\mapsto l_{\text{IV}}^{n}(\alpha)
is continuous, the set of feasible points
(l_{\text{IV}}^{n})^{-1}((-\infty,t])
is closed. Hence, our minimization problem is equivalent with the minimization of the continuous function
\alpha\mapsto l_{\text{OLS}}^{n}(\alpha)
over the convex and compact set
\overline{B(0,c)}\cap(l_{\text{IV}}^{n})^{-1}((-\infty,t])
. By the extreme value theorem, the minimum exist and is attainable. We conclude that the primal problem is solvable if
t>\inf_{\alpha}l_{\text{IV}}^{n}(\alpha)
.

By definition, Slater’s condition is satisfied if there exists a point in the relative interior of the problem domain where the constraint inequality is strict
(
Boyd and
Vandenberghe 2004
)
. Since the problem domain is

\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}+q_{1}}
, we need the existence of
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}+q_{1}}
such that
l^{n}_{\text{IV}}(\alpha)<t
. This is clearly satisfied if
t>\inf_{\alpha}l_{\text{IV}}^{n}(\alpha)
. Let us now specify the exact lower bound for the constraint bound as a function of the over-identifying restrictions.
Under- and just-identified case:
q_{2}\leq d_{1}
(
q\leq d_{1}+q_{1}
).
Assumption
Item

.(b)
yields that
\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times(d_{1}+q_{1})}
satisfies
\text{rank}(\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z})=q
. That is,
\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}
is of full row rank, hence surjective. Thus, we are guaranteed the existence of a
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}+q_{1}}
such that
\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}\tilde{\alpha}=\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Y}
, implying that
l_{\mathrm{IV}}^{n}(\tilde{\alpha})=0
.
Over-identified case:
d_{1}<q_{2}
(
d_{1}+q_{1}<q
).
Note that the constraint function
l_{\mathrm{IV}}^{n}(\alpha):\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}+q_{1}}\to\mathbb{R}
is strictly convex as the second derivative
D^{2}l_{\mathrm{IV}}^{n}(\alpha;\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z},\mathbf{A})\propto\mathbf{Z}^{\intercal}\mathbf{A}(\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{A})^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}
is
positive definite by the assumption that
\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times(d_{1}+q_{1})}
has full (column)
rank. The global minimum of
l_{\text{IV}}
is
therefore attained in the unique stationary point. Furthermore, the stationary point is found by solving the normal equation
Dl_{\mathrm{IV}}^{n}(\alpha;\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z},\mathbf{A})=0
. The solution to the normal equation is given by
\hat{\alpha}_{\text{TSLS}}^{n}=(\mathbf{Z}^{\intercal}P_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{Z})^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}^{\intercal}P_{\mathbf{A}}\mathbf{Y},

which is the standard TSLS estimator.

\square

Proof of
Theorem

3.2
:

Let
p_{\min}\in(0,1)
and let
3.6
and
3.7
hold. That is,
\mathbf{A}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}
and
\mathbf{Z}^{\intercal}\mathbf{Z}
are of full rank and
[\mathbf{Z}\,\,\mathbf{Y}]
is of full column rank. Furthermore, assume that
t_{n}^{\star}(p_{\min})>-\infty
and
T_{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\text{Pr}}^{n}(t_{n}^{\star}(p_{\min})))\leq Q_{\chi^{2}_{q}}(1-p_{\min})
.
First assume that
\hat{\alpha}_{\text{Pr}}^{n}(t_{n}^{\star}(p_{\min}))=\hat{\alpha}_{\text{OLS}}^{n}
. We note that

T_{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\text{OLS}}^{n})=T_{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\text{Pr}}^{n}(t_{n}^{\star}(p_{\min})))\leq Q_{\chi^{2}_{q}}(1-p_{\min}),

hence the global minimizer
\hat{\alpha}_{\text{OLS}}^{n}
of
\alpha\mapsto l_{\text{OLS}}^{n}(\alpha)
is unique, feasible and necessarily optimal in the PULSE problem, so
\hat{\alpha}_{\text{Pr}}^{n}(t_{n}^{\star}(p_{\min}))=\hat{\alpha}_{\text{OLS}}^{n}=\hat{\alpha}^{n}_{\text{PULSE}}
and we are done.

Now assume that
\hat{\alpha}_{\text{Pr}}^{n}(t_{n}^{\star}(p))\not=\hat{\alpha}_{\text{OLS}}^{n}
. Consider the PULSE problem of interest

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathrm{min}_{\alpha}&l_{\mathrm{OLS}}^{n}(\alpha)\\
\mathrm{subject\,to}&T_{n}(\alpha)\leq Q_{\chi^{2}_{q}}(1-p_{\min}),\end{array}

which is, in general, a non-convex quadratically constrained quadratic program. First we argue that the problem is solvable, i.e., the optimum is attainable.

To see this, let
p=p_{\min}
,
Q=Q_{\chi^{2}_{q}}(1-p_{\min})
and note that by the assumption
t_{n}^{\star}(p_{\min})>-\infty
we have that the feasible set of the PULSE problem is non-empty. By the assumptions that
[\mathbf{Z}\,\,\mathbf{Y}]
is of full column rank we have that
T_{n}(\alpha)
is well-defined for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}+q_{1}}
, as the denominator is never zero. By continuity of
\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}+q_{1}}\ni\alpha\mapsto T_{n}(\alpha)
we have that the feasible set
\mathcal{F}:=T_{n}^{-1}\left((-\infty,Q]\right),

is closed and non-empty, since it is the continuous preimage of a closed set. Applying the same arguments as seen earlier in the proof of
Lemma

3.2
, we know that
l_{\text{OLS}}^{n}(\alpha)\to\infty
when
\|\alpha\|\to\infty
. Hence, for a sufficiently large
c>0
we know that if
\alpha\not\in\overline{B(0,c)}
, where
\overline{B(0,c)}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}+q_{1}}
is the closed ball with centre 0 and radius
c
, then we only get suboptimal objective values
l_{\text{OLS}}^{n}(\alpha)>l_{\text{OLS}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\text{Pr}}^{n}(t_{n}^{\star}(p)))
. That is, we can without loss of optimality or loss of solutions restrict the feasible set to
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=T_{n}^{-1}((-\infty,Q])\cap\overline{B(0,c)}
a closed and bounded set in
\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}+q_{1}}
. Hence, by the extreme value theorem the minimum over
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
is guaranteed to be attained. That is, the PULSE problem is solvable.

However, by the non-convexity of
T_{n}
, the preimage
T_{n}^{-1}((-\infty,Q])
is in general not convex, so the minimum is not yet guaranteed to be attained in a unique point. We will show that the minimum of the PULSE problem is attained in a unique point, that exactly coincides with the primal PULSE solution. Fix any solution
\hat{\alpha}
to
the
PULSE problem and realize that the PULSE constraint is active in
\hat{\alpha}
,

\displaystyle T_{n}(\hat{\alpha})=Q.

(E.5)

This is seen by noting that
\hat{\alpha}_{\text{Pr}}^{n}(t_{n}^{\star}(p))\not=\hat{\alpha}_{\text{OLS}}^{n}
by assumption, so
\hat{\alpha}_{\text{OLS}}^{n}
is not feasible in the PULSE problem, that is,
\hat{\alpha}_{\text{OLS}}^{n}\not\in\mathcal{F}
.
If
\hat{\alpha}_{\text{OLS}}^{n}
was feasible, then
t_{n}^{\star}(p)=\sup\{t\in D_{\text{Pr}}:T_{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\mathrm{Pr}}^{n}(t))\leq Q_{\chi^{2}_{q}}(1-p_{\min})\}=l_{\text{IV}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}^{n}_{\text{OLS}}),
since
T_{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\text{Pr}}^{n}(l_{\text{IV}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}^{n}_{\text{OLS}}))=T_{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\text{OLS}}^{n})\leq Q
, hence

\hat{\alpha}_{\text{Pr}}^{n}(t_{n}^{\star}(p))=\argmin_{\alpha:l_{\text{IV}}^{n}(\alpha)\leq l_{\text{IV}}^{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{\text{OLS}}^{n})}l_{\text{OLS}}^{n}(\alpha)=\hat{\alpha}_{\text{OLS}}^{n},

which is a contradiction. That the optimum must be attained in a point, where the PULSE inequality constraint is active then follows from
Lemma

F.11
of
Appendix

F

and the conclusion above that the only stationary point of
l_{\text{OLS}}^{n}
,
\hat{\alpha}_{\text{OLS}}^{n}
, is not feasible.

Thus,
